I don’t care if his specialty is supposedly orthopedics, does Sparks not realize how much medical school this character would have had to go through before even touching his specialty of Ortho?! I’m, like, insulted FOR this guy. The injustice that Sparks has created for him!!!!! Am I asking for too much? I just want an author to actually know what they’re talking about when they write about specific subjects. What a weirdly unsatisfying book (not to mention deeply problematic - more on that at the bottom of this rant). I've read plenty of JG over the years, and have come to expect a level of clunky exposition, even clunkier dialogue, and mostly-wooden characters. But the counterbalance to those shortfalls is an old-fashioned potboiler, with a great central character, one or two interesting collaborators, and a web of intrigue that you know will be untangled by the penultimate chapter...you just can't quite see how. This book was a big letdown on all those fronts! First, it's not remotely clear who Jake Brigance is anymore - he does at least a dozen things throughout the book that are craaaazy unethical, to the point where you wonder if this is going to be a low-key Breaking Bad situation (which would be cool!), but then not a single one of those things gets addressed in any meaningful way. And while some old faces float in and out to say some wise words, the only new, interesting character - a Scotswoman who runs a children's criminal defense non-profit - mostly just sits around and marvels at Jake's supposed (but entirely unapparent) brilliance. Aside from the deceased abusive cop, JG doesn't seem to have the stomach to make anyone a "bad guy" (the prosecutor's a nice dude, the cops are respectful, even the thuggish family of the deceased are just acting out of anguish), so there's no real tension as the trial date nears. Meanwhile the B and C plots - a secondary big-dollar civil trial, and a profoundly unethical and gross adoption scheme by Jake and Carla - just sort of meander along, distracting from the heart of the story, and then are resolved, with no tension or conflict, in the final pages. But most frustrating was the centerpiece trial finale which was, simply, a dud. The big drama of the trial is explained in detail a quarter of the way through the book, then discussed ad nauseum (as in "gee whiz, when X happens, that's really gonna create some big drama at trial!"), then it plays out exactly as the characters imagined. There's a lengthy section about jury selection, but virtually none of the "dream jurors" get picked, which makes the arrival of sudden jury drama in the final pages all the more disorienting. All told, I did pick up steam in terms of grinding through this thing when the trial began, but it was the most dissatisfying trial sequence I can recall among the dozens of JG books I've read over the years... I'm a firm believer in assessing books on their own terms - JG is not a literary giant, he just writes good old fashioned thrillers. But even on those terms, this book was a hot mess! ***Finally, I wanted to make sure to unpack this book from a "good read" perspective first, before getting into the messed up politics, because even setting aside the latter, it is decidedly NOT a good read. But there is a subplot - a 14 y/o girl is raped, impregnated by the rapist, and then (along with her indigent, desperate mother) is urged to/coerced into/forced into having the baby in order to (a) benefit her brother's trial, and (b) allow the main character to adopt the baby - that is fucked up and not even a little okay. I could see a scenario where all those things actually come to pass, where the sticky politics of Mississippi circa 1990 lead the characters to do the things they do - but since JG seems no longer able to write with any level of nuance or human insight, it lands like some Handmaids Tale horseshit.***

The most glaring problem to me, though, is the covert (and sometimes overt) sexism. Women are frequently described in terms of appearances (vs. descriptions of male character’s successes/actions...was it really necessary to describe how Libby looked, or to include Jake’s perspective that she is *almost* attractive...gross!), and any successes women enjoy (as with Portia’s character) are *because* of male support/assistance. Carla (who is a teacher with her own profession) is often found in the kitchen, preparing food or coffee for the men, dismissively does “girl stuff” (p. 297), and hangs on the fringe of Jake’s team and only knows about courtroom/legal affairs “because she was married to a criminal defense lawyer” (p. 354). The Gambles are also treated as token poor people in need of saving by the strapping Brigance, rather than as actual human beings. Won’t even get into issues of race or the abortion debate and how the Brigances pressure the poor, poor Gambles into taking on their perspective on abortion. Ugh. This novel actually put me off reading for two weeks. It takes a major insult to do that, and this one is a major insult. Among other things, it wraps its anti-abortion message in a warm and fuzzy blanket, so unless one is a careful reader, as I am, this anti-abortion theme is easily missed. Make no mistake, this is not a crime novel but another chapter in Louise Penny’s moral crusade. But she hides her moral crusade within a blanket of half truths. Furthermore, although she appears, on the surface, to be supporting left-wing causes, she is actually right-wing in most of her views, especially in her anti-abortion stand. Normally, when I don’t like a book, I simply stop reading it. But this one made me so angry that I couldn’t read at all. I wanted to finish it so I could write a good review of all the sneaky ways Penny uses to get her right-wing views sound acceptable, and I did almost complete it, by reading a few chapters every couple of days, almost finishing it before the library book had to be returned. She invents a convenient fabrication that is so off-the-wall fascist (i.e., Professor Robinson’s views) that no one could fault her for opposing it, but it is a science fiction situation, one that could only exist within a totalitarian government, and Canada is not a totalitarian country. She uses this fabricated situation as a “straw man” within which to place her fuzzy, warm arguments for a strong right-wing family environment. She also uses some old, real-life fascist experiments to bolster her moral crusade (the 1950 Cameron experiments done at McGill University, used to treat schizophrenic medical patients). She ignores real-life, right-wing situations that could be examined within the framework of her Three Pines community, such as the anti-vax movement, because it is messy, with no easy answers. With the anti-vax movement, no matter what stand her hero, Gamache, took, he could not remain a god — and Penny definitely considers him a god, the saviour of families. What made me so angry was that she was able to fool so many readers into believing that she was on a moral crusade against right-wing ideology when she was really pushing her own right-wing concepts — anti-abortion concepts, family patriarch concepts.

Just as real people, characters in books can evolve and not necessarily in a good way. Where I enjoyed Gamache’s company as he was a gentleman detective in the charming village of Three Pines, he has turned into a smug, pedantic pater familias who thrives on sheer devotion from his surroundings and he expects everyone to bow to his demands. About everything irked me in this book and certainly the writing style. I think it is ok to want to emphasise a certain detail once by cutting a sentence short and adding a single or a few words for effect. But it is downright irritating if you do it all the time. Literally. All. The. Time. Yes, pun intended. Also the use of the French words or turns of phrases that aren’t always accurate are annoying. In previous books I would think that it could be a Canadian thing, but when in Paris the characters are supposed to speak correctly. The storyline is sordid and unbelievable. An employee that leaves a computer at the office? And her boss knows her password? The best policeman of the world (according to Gamache) is bullied by a security agent? I love the characters that Louise Penny has created in the Chief Inspector Armand Gamache series and look forward to spending nights with them as they hue to a moral code where truth, honesty, service toward others and family are revered. Reading the back of the Barnes and Noble special edition, where Penny similarly comes off as a thankful, gracious individual, it therefore pains me to rate this book one-star. In fact, it was closer to five stars until the end of the book, where Penny decides to go for a happy ending instead of a realistic one. I will not reveal any spoilers but for the sake of the literary integrity I have to rate this one star and admonish Penny for taking the easy way out--one that preserves the Gamache franchise so that it can continue to serve as a bastion where "all the angels" can continue to live in Three Pines. Certainly the characters in this series have suffered mightily, and in these times, it is comforting to read about families, friends and neighbors who achieve a hard-won happy ending. Penny is writing fiction, and she is entitled to create these deus ex machina events whenever she wants, but these authorial miracles do a disservice to literature and the characters she has created, and ultimately readers. Yes, there are good people. Yes, there is often light to be found in the darkest of situations and brave men and women who don't succumb to what is easy. But it almost seems that these last second graces that occur in tense situations during Penny's novels has become a contrivance that actually annoys and ultimately upsets the reader. Why? When one can empathize with the humanness of characters(including the individuals in our own lives whom we love and will eventually die), it feels like we need to be protected from grieving and pretend that all will be well until the next installment of the franchise arrives. Such characters, including their readers, deserve the honesty of reality and the ability to cherish their humanness, and not be protected from unpleasant feelings. The holism of great literature demands that death be explored so that a full understanding of life can be understood. Without this willingness to confront the realistic expectations of the scenes she creates, she is short-changing her characters and her readers as a result.

I can not believe this was a Pulitzer finalist…it felt like reading an eighth grade writing assignment.

Percival Everett takes one of the greatest tales ever told and, first, sucks the whole life out of it and fills it with dull, boring anger and resentment. He takes the wonder of a young boy seeing the world for the first time and trying to understand his place in it and the role he is to, and could, play in it, and replaces it with the insipid morality of his main character. He takes one of the most beautiful-hearted characters in literature - Jim - and turns him into a disdainful intellectual who has somehow, in between his backbreaking labour as a slave in antebellum America, managed to read and interpret the political philosophies of Voltaire and Locke, enough to hold vapid imaginary debates with them. Then he proceeds to rinse out all the adventure from the story and create a plodding account of escape from slavery, full of simplistic plot progression that borders on Gary Stu wish fulfillment. In the process, he fills Jim - now, self-importantly, James, on account of he can debate social equality Voltaire, don’t you know - with, at best, disinterest, and at worst, disdain for young Huck Finn, a gentle, kind-hearted boy who looks to him for advice and connection. Halfway through, though, he decides to abandon this whole affair, jettisons Huck unceremoniously, and sets James off on a “daring” escape from his miserable condition. After a few specious and unconvincing hijinks during which he introduces, and then conveniently dispenses with, various half-developed side characters, he brings Huck back again. At which point he decides that this whole thing should actually be a soap opera and introduces one of the most cringeworthy “twists” I’ve ever come across in a storytelling initiative, right up there with the Pam Ewing dream sequence from Dallas and an inadvertent send-up of the most famous line from Star Wars. Oh, and if the storytelling and plot aren’t absurd enough, the characters are all one-dimensional, most being introduced just to be easily discarded without adding anything to the narrative or helping in the development of Jim’s - I’m sorry, James’ - character. From Sadie and Lizzie to Norman and Sammy, with brief cameos by Easter, Duncan Emmett, Brock and Katie, they all blink in and out of existence momentarily for no discernible reason whatsoever. And then he ends with some Django Unchained-esque violent denouement which is supposed to bring to a head the anger and despair that the titular character feels, a suitably ferocious climax to this tale of putative redemption. I’m sad to say that I felt nothing for James. I felt neither his pain nor his helplessness, neither the desperation nor impotent rage of the condition of slavery, nor the catharsis in his expiation at the end. This was a nothingburger of a book, a pointless, unnecessary, incompetent travesty of an attempt at trying to tell a beloved classic from another point of view.

** spoiler alert ** When I heard about this book I was so captivated it motivated me to finally read Huck Finn so I could properly appreciate this retelling. By the end of the book I was looking for any reason to give it something other than 1 star, but I couldn't do it. Other than the idea, there wasn't anything I enjoyed about it. This book is bad, my first 1 star review bad (I dont rate my DNFs). This was a bad book for a lot of reasons, including.... The Characters James/Jim - James felt like an obnoxious college english or philosophy major that wont shut up about the latest book he was assigned. He somehow was more educated and well read than the average American today, but that passion only ever showed in imaginary vapid debates with Voltaire and Locke. He was an extremely one dimensional character. His interesting moments of growth in the original, such as beating his daughter for not being responsive, then immediately realizing his wickedness, were scrapped. Jim was the exact same character at the beginning and end of the novel. Huck - The only character with even a little bit of growth, but it was only a sad reflection of the original novel. His biggest moments of development were muddied, he was anti-slavery way too early given the environment he grew up in, and the twist at the end (Ill return to this later), completely ruins the point of the original novel. The Women - They served no other purpose than to be raped, kidnapped, abused, or murdered, all to fuel Jame's rage. Zero character development, just simple rage inducing plot devices. The White People - Every white person in this was basically a character from Django Unchained. They were all stupid, racist, and evil with zero chance of any growth or an ounce of character depth. Yes, slave owners were bad people, that doesnt mean they cant be interesting. The Rest - There wasn't a well written character in this novel. Emmett almost was, but then he just devolved into every other white character. Without using Twain's work, I cant describe a single character trait of anyone in this book. The Writing The language in this book is extremely simple. 95% of the novel is dialogue with almost no descriptions, and the descriptions we get are nothing more than basic observations. This wouldn't be so bad if the dialogue was well written, but it read like a high school writing assignment. Here are some of my favorite examples: "You mean you was pullin' on my leg?" I said. He was enjoying himself and that was all right with me. It always made life easier when white folks could laugh at a poor slave now and again. "I had you goin'," Huck said. I acted like he'd hurt my feelings. White people love feeling guilty. "I'm sorry, Jim. I just thought it was funny," he said. "Yeah, it be funny, Huck, sho nuff funny." I pushed out my lower lip a bit, an expression I displayed only for white people. The author has zero faith in the reader, he constantly reminds us that everything Jim does is an act for the sake of white people. I heard rustling in the trees. It sounded like a human, so I had to assume it was a white human. "What dere be in dem woods..." Just remove 90% of this exposition and replace it with some world building or character complexity to make the book feel less sterile. Here is another gem right after Sammy's death "Do we bury her?" "Do you believe in God?" I asked. "I suppose I do," he said. "I don't. But maybe Sammy did. So, yes, we'll bury her. Isn't that what people who believe in God want?" "I don't know." Literally any time religion is mentioned in this book, James has to make some dumbass comment about how he doesn't believe in religion. He is basically that one friend that just turned away from the church and wont shut up about how great atheism is. Also Jim is well read to the point he can school Voltaire and Locke in a debate in a single sentence, but doesn't know that people want to be buried when they die? Give me a break. The Ending By far the worst part of a terrible novel. While the original novel has it's problems and is definitely showing it's age at times, the point was that it took the mind of a child to grasp the wickedness of slavery. Humans are capable of terrible atrocities, and the power of societal influence is sobering. Huck was the perfect perspective since he was mature enough to have developed plenty of racist beliefs, but young enough to eventually see the absurdity of them. He needs to be white, because the book is about moving forward, it's about the next generation. By making Huck Jim's son, you further make the book more of an us vs them story. That's to say nothing of the execution. We are in Jim's mind the entire book and not even once does he acknowledge internally that Huck is his son? Nobody noticed that Huck is a little darker and maybe he's half black? Nothing more than a single observation by Tom that his hair is thicker? Terrible, lazy writing. We also have this legendary exchange when Huck finds out "Why are you talking like that?" "Are you referring to my diction or my content?" Nobody talks like this, especially to a barely literate 12 year old. The book reads like I am very smart fan fiction. Maybe Jim is doing it intentionally to flex his intelligence over Huck, but that's even worse. If you have to demean a child to feel better about yourself, you're an asshole. Multiply that by ten if the child is your own son. Overall this book reads like a horrible cross between Twain's work and Django Unchained. It's lacking all the humor and complexity of the original, and all fun from the latter. I truly don't understand the reviews raving about this book. It really is that bad.

I’ve been trying to get through this book since last summer, and THANK GOD ON HIGH I finally finished it last night. Given that Wicked was popular with both critics and lay people, AND the fact that there’s a wildly successful stage play associated with it, I figured this book couldn’t possibly disappoint. And yet… First, the themes/motifs were all. over. the. place. Animal rights, “otherness”, gender, good v. evil, and religion were the major players underlying the plot. And when I say underlying I mean thinly veiled by a meandering story. I kept thinking, he’s not really talking about animal rights or religion. He wouldn’t be that obvious. But he is. So much so that at times the story appeared to be merely a vehicle for positing his theories on that laundry list of Big Questions. Even then, I’m not sure what his theories are. It all seemed a bit pointless. I never could figure out where he stood on any of the issues, except for animal rights. He also seemed to jump from one theme to the next without any clear transition. Like the animal rights issue. It’s all over the first half of the book and then you don’t hear one single word about it until the last 40 pages or so. It’s like he thought, “oh yeah! I still need one more animal right allusion to make quota..." Second, this book was toooooooooooooo long. The editor should have worked harder to reign in Gregory Maguire’s meanderings. I know I used that word about three sentences ago, but that’s what it felt like. He was rambling through his thoughts with me as the unfortunate tag-along. Finally, this book told me absolutely nothing (save a few gossip-worthy details) about the Wicked Witch of the West that I couldn’t have figured out on my own. If it had ever crossed my mind to consider how the Witch ended up so Wicked, I could’ve saved myself the 406 pages and chalked her behavior up to the classic little girl lost. Poor WWW, she was so misunderstood and mistreated. Now she doesn’t know how to behave as an adult because she grew up with a very unsatisfying home life. It’s the story half of the current prison population could blame their troubles on, and quite frankly I expected more out of a book that received so much critical praise. My advice: See the musical. I hear it’s fantastic. But then again, that’s what they said about the book.

I don't even know where to start. This book -- actually, the concept of this book -- had so much promise. This could've been a great revisionist retelling of the Wizard of Oz. Yes, it fleshed out Oz itself - what a rich land, people by various creatures: humans, animals and everything in between. The political and religious strata of Oz was well-thought out. But I couldn't connect to any of the characters, especially the main character of Elphaba. I felt that none of them were fleshed out. None of them were likable, either in a positive or negative way. Sometimes, you're drawn to characters who are so evil because they're interesting. You become interested in their back story (and there is always one) and what makes them tick. Of course, almost everyone is drawn to the hero of the story, be they good or flawed, regardless of whether they're the hero-type or the underdog. But in my mind, while Elphaba was the center of the story, there was just nothing about her that drew me in, nothing that made me want to know her better. Nothing tugged at my heart strings or made me go "Grrr...I can't stand her!" Maguire proselytized. Ad nauseum. To the point where I saw no point in going further with the book. While I can see that this work would appeal to some people who revel in exposition and live and breathe religious and political polemic (there are die-hard Wicked fans out there -- of the book, I mean, which is significantly different from the musical, and that has a large fan base as well) but sad to say, I am not one of them (a fan of the book). I think there's a place for everything, and when I pick up a book that purports to be about the life and times of the Wicked Witch of the West, I expect a fantastical backstory about her. And that's the thing. I know very little about her. She's green, but why is she green? She's got normal parents, but how did she become this freakish green baby with shark teeth? You know about her parents' dalliances, their religious fervor, their sexual proclivities, their societal vagaries. But you don't see how this shaped Elphaba. There are so many holes in the story, so many unanswered questions, and whenever I expected to find an answer, there were one or two vague, often nebulous, non-answers. The story meandered all over the place, dropping characters here and there into the narrative -- characters that (one hopes) will enrich the characterization of Elphaba's life. Sometimes they did; most times, they didn't. About a quarter of the way through, I wondered where this was all heading. About halfway through, I wanted to rip my hair out and beg for something -- anything -- to happen that would make me feel connected to our heroine or the story. But there was nothing. Not even a clock dragon to crawl into. This novel could've done so much. It held so much promise. So much. But it failed to deliver, and at least for me, it was a supreme letdown.

Or at the very least, was not edited: Obsessive compulsive disorder is three separate words. How did obsessivecompulsive get through the editors? A simple rule like adding a line space between dialogue to show that a different person is speaking has been ignored at least twice in this book. Was it even edited?? The correct term for disassociation is dissociation. Some general plot/character development issues: Emmy making up stories in her head and treating them like evidence and fact. Supposed to be a trained officer but constantly needs daddy to tell her what to do next in a tone that should be reserved for 5 year olds. Tampers with evidence so many times it’s a joke. Her mother with apparent late stage alzheimers, but is still mobile, having logical conversations, knows how to turn on a stove. Irish twins are born within 12 months of each other, not 14 months apart. What’s with the obsession with the cars in this book? Feels like every page has a detailed description of a vehicle. Cut that detail out and the length of the book would have been halved. What older woman would file someone’s name incorrectly in her address book, purely based on a nickname given to them by local teenagers? She wouldn’t have known the nickname when she hired him and that’s when she would have written his contact details in her book. In their little story time session where they make up the details 12 years later, why call the perpetrator the Bad Guy, rather than simply the perpetrator? DNF. My conscience and convictions disqualify me from continuing this book. Same-sex romance is an instant DNF for me. Disappointing, but on to something else. *Edit* To clarify for those intolerant of Christian values: It is quite a bit different in intentionality when Sanderson mentioned same-sex relationships in previous books. What I mean is that, yes, there was a passing reference to a couple in Oathbringer, and you can definitely see Renarin's preferences. The differences lie where in Oathbringer, he mentioned this to make the world seem more realistic in scope. For everyone saying that I "don't want homosexuals to exist," that's absurd. I don't see an issue in referencing that homosexuality is real in the same way that I don't have an issue when Sanderson talks about abuse in his books. It's real.....but it isn't glorified or promoted. What I can't, in good conscience, permit is when the promotion of this relationship becomes a prominent plot point, story line, or focal point. I don't want to be entertained by the promotion of sexual sin, just like I don't want to be entertained by the promotion of blasphemy. Sanderson even admitted that he was taking a risk in that regard by including it. A risk that, from my worldview, was not worth taking

Might as well have been making pop culture references it was so immersion breaking for me. The Wokelight Archives That book sucked

In 2016 I was in a relationship with someone I cared for very deeply. She cheated on me, and I had a lot of trouble falling asleep for several months. Benadryl wasn’t enough to quiet my mind, but Sanderson’s audiobooks (The Stormlight Archive in particular), played quietly on a Bluetooth speaker with a timer, distracted me enough to let me sleep. These have been important books to me, and I wanted very much to like this one. But it’s just really bad — Sanderson’s bludgeoning prose, the jokes, the sudden injection of 21st century language, the Reddit/high school freshman level philosophical musings, the insistence on sending all important characters on side quests, the lack of any new character development, the rehashing of already-resolved storylines, the heavy-handed treatment of autism, gay romance, inter-species romance, etc. It doesn’t work, and felt like reading fanfic. Chapter by chapter I found myself increasingly bored and apathetic, willing to look up spoilers, and very ready to be done reading this very tedious book.

This book was a massive disappointment. I have read everything Brando Sando has ever written, most of it multiple times and loved almost all of it, but this was just awful. Obviously, spoiler warning. Part 1, problems with the plot: 1. If you remove every POV from Shallan/Rlain/Renarin does the plot change at all? Nope, nothing they did contributed to the ending in any way. The entire ghostbloods/mishram storyline was basically pointless, not to mention boring. Oh no! Emotional issues that should have been resolved last book! You’re back! Should I free the openly genocidal demigod? Yes, because… oh I actually can’t think of a good reason, but there is one, I promise. Will there be any consequences from freeing the genocidal demigod? Nope! Everything works out just fine! Tbh if we had just not seen anything from them until the end, when Shallan gets trapped in the cognitive realm, then they show all of this stuff as flashbacks in book six, while mishram is causing problems, these scenes would be much better. Because you would actually care about what happened, because it would provide information about what’s currently happening. 2. The Adolin storyline was the only genuinely good storyline, but the ending of it was horrible. The “un-oathed” twist seemed like such deux ex machina. Brandon broke all three of his rules of magic with this one, but especially #1: “An author’s ability to solve conflict with magic is DIRECTLY PROPORTIONAL to how well the reader understands said magic.” Adolin should have become a radiant. That would have been a better more satisfying ending, that actually fits in with the current magic system. Adolin’s headspace was also really frustrating during the ending. His obsession with there being a difference between promises and oaths seemed ridiculous when an apocalypse is going on, and I thought it would have been much more satisfying for him to forgive his father, then swear some oaths and become a radiant. I was waiting for it the whole book, only to have it stolen away so some random characters I don’t really care about can get plates and blades. One last criticism on this topic: All due respect to Adolin as a duelist, but a guy with one leg, who just fought for hours and hours on a battlefield, somehow beats a full shard bearing fused with a candlestick? Nah. 3. We didn’t need all of those flashbacks in the spiritual realm. Leaving aside the fact that the spiritual realm feels way less impressive and way more gimmicky than it’s been hyped up as this entire time, we could have gotten the exact same information if all of the flashbacks had been eliminated and we just got the tanavast POV scenes. It was also really frustrating on every battlefield for them to be losing so badly when dalinar’s presence and Stormlight could have helped them win literally every battle. Not to mention that the Stormfather could have just told Dalinar all of this stuff from the beginning, without them needing to waste the entire book getting exposition that they DONT EVEN USE. 4. Losing Thaylena(hope that’s spelled right, I’m an audiobook person) should be important, but it doesn’t feel that way. The important thing is that Jasnah lost an argument and now she feels bad about it. 5. Moash. I’m annoyed that Navani finally punished him, and he finally had to feel bad about what he had done, just for neither of those things to matter whatsoever. The man was broken, blinded, overwhelmed by guilt and suffering because he had murdered his friend, then less than a week later he’s just out there doing it again, no consequences except now he can see spren! 5.5 Sigzil got done dirty in this book just to force him to into the sunlit man. 6. The big one, Kaladin. This man tried to end his own life literally 1 day ago, at the end of RoW. It feels pretty absurd for him to be Therapist(Tm) one day later, not to mention that there is an apocalypse and a war going on, like bro, all due respect, but what exactly do you think your character was developing toward? “I will protect” *leaves everyone to protect themselves* I did like that he became a herald, but I didn’t like how it happened. Especially after Szeth was built up towards it, I always knew it would be Kaladin instead, but it was literally just because Szeth was knocked out? And soup and music over ~a week is supposed to cure Szeth? What was the point of going to each monastery in turn? Just to drag the plot out so it takes enough time? Kaladin’s sections have always been my favorite, but this time it just dragged. I have more criticism but it will go in the writing section. 7. Finally the ending. A main question of the books has been is it okay to kill to protect? That’s been a mainstream theme of the entire book, and is the focal question of Kaladin’s entire character. But then when the time comes to answer it definitively and let the consequences fall where they may, Brandon cops out, dodges the question, and refuses to commit. Dalinar, the blackthorn, legendary warrior, refuses to kill one person to protect millions, if not billions. And his sacrifice is for naught, because taravangian resurrects his ghost anyway. 8. Oh and some older wiser super god pauses time to chat with Dalinar before he makes his final decision?? What is that guy doing, and if hes so old and wise and smart, why doesn't he come and solve some of the problems. It felt implied that Nohadon is adonalsium, so why is he just letting the bad guys win all the time bro? Its not as big a problem for this book, but its going to be a massive problem for future books. Part 2, the writing: Two of the three most interesting characters in the entire series on a quest to find and recruit a mad demigod, accompanied by another mad demigod, fighting duels with superpowered immortal beings mind controlling an entire nation is somehow boring???? How do you mess this one up that bad? 1. Modernisms: Nothing destroys immersion faster or harder than modern day ideas, words, phrases, or messaging. It is impossible to stay immersed in the story when words like therapy, racist, shit, genetics, dating “back in fifteen”(like everyone has clocks that measure minutes???) etc. are being thrown around constantly. Therapy as a concept is basically a post ww2 invention. So having completely modern therapy techniques and ideas pumped into everything is utterly ludicrous. It absolutely destroys any sense of antiquity, immersion, or gravitas in the world when all the characters seem to be worried about are modern problems and themes. I’m reading this book to get away from modern political and social themes, not to wallow in them. 2. I don’t need to know how every character is feeling at every single moment for the entire book. Show me what they do and say, and let me draw my own conclusions. I don’t need 3 paragraphs of explanation of their every feeling every time they do something. It’s exhausting, boring and makes the book lose flow. Let me make some inferences about the character! If you explain every single thought, it actually makes the character feel less deep, less real. The more dimensions of a character you tell me about, instead of showing me actions and letting me guess, the less there seem to be. My imagination about their motives will always let me relate to the character more than you telling me about every single motivation they have ever had. Less is more, and in this case, more was way way less. This is why Shallan drags so much as a character, we have had too much insight into her inner thoughts for way too many pages/hours across too many books. 3. Just straight up bad prose: Go read the intro to ch 24. It’s a very prosey section where Szeth is dancing, then for no reason at all we have this line: “Five feet across and three feet high—at least the part that emerged from the soil—“. Bro what? Why include that? It wrecks the flow of the scene completely! I was listening, and I remember being jarringly brought right back into the real world by that one line, disrupting the story, breaking the immersion. 4. Too many POVs, too frequent POV changes. I don’t think I need to say anymore about this. 5. Sexualization characters. Why did we feel the need to mention that Syl has human anatomy? Are her and Kaladin going to have a physical relationship? Like whats the point here? It feels very fanservicey. The same with the Adolin Shallan shower scene. This is not a romance novel, don't make it one. I don't mind when characters are obviously in a physical relationship, I'm a huge fan of the wheel of time, but the way it is done is just not it. Part 3, Vibes: 1. Preachy: Lots of scenes where Brandon has a goal of a message he wants to get across, and instead of showing, tells us exactly what message he wants us to understand. Eg. Kaladin roasts librarian lady for being mean to Syl. 2. Not hard hitting: I don’t spend much time on reddit or reading theories or anything like that, until recently I havent spent much time on sanderson related YouTube, so I didn’t get any spoilers about stuff like Shallan’s mom, or Gav as Odium's champion etc. But even though I had no idea, they still did not make a big impact. I remember being surprised, but instead of that surprise sticking with me, it felt hollow. Maybe its because I didn’t actually care who the champion would be, or who Shallan’s mom was? But I don’t think there were any surprises in the whole book that actually hit hard. 3. Too much explanation: I want to know stuff, but he gave us too much stuff. This book felt like he had a prequel written about the heralds (which sounds totally dope), and he just cut random chapters out and gave it to us, but it didn’t actually give us any deeper understanding of HOW they did stuff. We got more about what they did, but not really how or why. 4. Boring. I could not care less about huge sections of this book, the plot, the writing, the character development, all just fall flat. 5. Felt very YA. I came here for an adult epic fantasy, not a YA romance stormlight fanfic. 6. Too long, and I like long books, but not when they are full of this crap. Part 4, politics: What makes great stories great is that they are relevant in any time period. Lord of the Rings was written almost a century ago, and it is so enduring that people are still making movies and TV shows about it. Eternal concepts like good vs evil, order vs chaos, duty, honor, suffering, and self sacrifice, will always be mainstays of good storytelling. Themes like these are what made the first 2 books so good. What will not be a mainstay of great storytelling is hypermodern gender, queer, and therapy themes. People will still be reading books like the wheel of time(which has progressive themes, just not at the expense of the plot) and the lord of the rings in 50-100 years. Up until this book I was sure that the SA would join them, and some of the books still might. However I fear WaT will join the likes of other politically active stories from hugely popular storylines that quickly become irrelevant and forgotten because the focus shifted away from good writing in favor of political messaging. Its the same reason that Christian entertainment has done so poorly for so many years, because the message comes at the expense of the story. One last point about the political messaging is that once again it breaks the immersion. In the Wheel of Time, progressive themed characters felt natural and organic, part of the world, the same way they are part of historical events in our world. In WaT, they felt like a lecture from a gender studies major at a private university. Whether or not you are in favor of it, it breaks the immersion. Conclusion: I love the Stormlight Archive, up until WaT it was my favorite series, and I would recommend Sanderson to everyone I knew. I cannot do that anymore. The first two books are phenomenal, the third is kind of boring early on but sticks the landing, I really like RoW and everything that we learn and the character arcs. WaT is an unmitigated disaster. It's depressing for me to have to write this, because I love Sanderson as a storyteller, but this isn't it. His editors and pre-readers did a horrible job, and I can't help but feel that political messaging was put above storytelling. I highly doubt that the story I just read was the one he imagined when he wrote TWoK 15 years ago.

** spoiler alert ** Sanderson, please, please, hire another editor! I came to hear about healing a mad herald, find out why the Shin were silent, and hear how the tense Final Ten Days go for celebtrities and common folk alike. Instead, I hear tiktok-quality praise to the glory of the spren of meetings and committees. Let's gloss over how Shallan and Jasnah cannot function without a strong male counterpart. I accept healing isn't always going forward, but...come on, why is Jasnah nerfed? And don't get me Started on how being a Ghostblood grants you unlimited plot armour. If anyone but a Ghostblood had tossed that dagger in, d'you reckon it'd utterly destroy a perpendicularity and singularity-tug everyone in? P.S. I was unprepared for so many fanfics to be written by a legit author. Had to check the cover to ensure this wasn't Twilight DLC.

This is a frustrating review for me to write. For a long time Brandon Sanderson has been my favorite modern fantasy author, and the Stormlight Archives my favorite unfinished series, but after reading this book that has changed for me. While parts of this book are absolutely incredible, and it doesn't change how much I love the first three books, there are several issues I have with Wind and Truth that I just can't overlook, and they ruined the reading experience for me overall. The first issue, and by far the most detrimental to how I view Wind and Truth, is the same-sex romance agenda that Sanderson places front and center. While he's included passing references at homosexuality in some of his past books, in Wind and Truth, these same-sex relationships are championed by some of the main characters, and it is heavily glorified. Due to my convictions, and my conscience, I started to skip each section where these characters would show up once I realized what was going on, but I'm incredibly disappointed that Sanderson chose to include this. It was completely unnecessary, and makes it so I can't recommend this book. Other issues I had include the way that Sanderson tries to write humor. A lot of the jokes sound like they're coming straight from a group of middle school boys, which quickly loses its charm and feels jarring to read in this kind of epic series. Likewise, Sanderson's attempt to address "mental illness" in a fantasy book where people wear super suits and kill stuff with magical swords comes off as shallow at best and preachy at worst. It seriously feels like he took a psychology textbook and copy pasted some sections into this book. Taken together, these issues compile to make one very frustrating read. Were there some awesome parts? Yes. Does the ending absolutely blow your mind? Yes. Will I ever read it again? Nope.

I sense intense anxiety, bordering on paranoia, from Jack Carr’s latest “work.” His preface is this long spew about the USA’s withdrawal Afghanistan, the military budget (he claims 50% of the military budget and 70% of the intelligence budget goes to defense contactors, but I would love to know where he got those numbers from), the politicians in DC (I am guessing these are not the same politicians that recently VOTED AGAINST the PACT ACT you know, the one that would assist veterans impacted by the BURN PUTS?!)), companies tracking us all the time (turn off your cookies and use incognito mode, dummie), Silicon Valley, social media, trolls (those negative reviews getting to you, Jackie?), THE LEFT, triggered critics (basically anyone who did not kiss Carr’s soggy cheeks and praise his work), military officers (especially those who write and pass legislation that will serve their donors and investments well, which honestly, same. I hate Dan Crenshaw too) and especially the insiders versus the outsiders. Jack Carr/James Reece places himself on the outsider but it’s like – Jack Carr hangs out with the dudes from Black Rifle Coffee on the regular, has been on Fox News and Tucker Carlson multiple times, and gets to speak at conferences and events like the Ronald Raegan Library. Jack Carr is the insider. Jack Carr has the megaphone and can spew out this political treatise disguised as a book. There is also this obsession with really bloody, really nasty deaths. I am putting in spoiler tags to hide it because this is very upsetting and nasty, but there are some long descriptions and depictions of the following: (view spoiler) Jack Carr’s mind is in a very dark place. The book is just bad. Bad. Bad. Bad. I hated The Terminal List, but this is just much worse. Even (some) fans who wrote they enjoyed the previous books seemed to have soured in this book. Jack Carr could not realistic dialogue if he had a gun to his head, and that gun was held by Mark Twain. James Reece’s dialogue is about 50% exposition, 40% repeating what someone said back to them, 10% is untreated PTSD spewing out of him. Jack Carr also has this very annoying habit repeating facts and little tidbits two, three, sometimes four times over (let’s trot out the 1972 Munich Olympics massacre, because that somehow ties into the plot! Did you know that nicotine is absorbed into the bloodstream? Did you know Jack Carr is freaked out by algorithms!? Well you will know all that and more by the end of this book)! The plot, whatever it was, is the same as it was in the previous books in that James Reece is emotionally manipulated by those around them to kill people for the hell of it. And along the way he pines after Katie, thinks about Laura and Lucy (remember them? Neither do I), and ruminates over his dad, Thomas (because of course this has to be some sort of legacy family). If you have read any book in this series, then you know how it goes. James Reece sails on through, taking hits and bullets like a Terminator, dealing out torture and murder willy-nilly. Along the way product placement are dropped like really bad diarrhea – one long, stinky steam that never seems to end and leaves you feeling drained and shaky and wondering where you went wrong. There is this odd emphasis on Russian Spetsnaz. My favorite character was Oleg, who sadly, does not get to survive the book. Oleg is sent to kill James Reece (even though Oleg is 62, or 52, I can’t remember and I already returned the library book) and this action and subsequent torture sequence is used for filler. You could cut it out completely and NOTHING would change. I wanted to know more about Oleg. How did he meet his wife (whose name we never get, btw)? How did he get along with the step-kids (the implication seems to be that he got along fine with them)? How did he start his gym? What was the culture shock moving from Russia to Italy? What was his favorite foods? What did Oleg do on his days off? What was Oleg's workout routine? This character reminds me of this anonymous diarist who was a Russian Spetsnaz fighting in Chechnya. I cannot find the full diary translated online, but we are lucky enough to have excerpts and this is such a rich, dark, complex text that blows Jack Carr's completely out of the water. And when I think of Oleg, I think of this dude and what he was going through. How come this book wasn’t James Reece teaming up with Oleg on some sort of mission, two aging veterans from different pasts and backgrounds but sharing the experience of fighting a losing war in Afghanistan going on an adventure together. I thought about it, then realized THAT WOULD HAVE TAKEN TALENT THAT JACK CARR DOESN’T POSSESS. Oleg fails in his mission (because James Reece is not allowed to fail) and wow was his punishment brutal. Oleg is tortured with drugs and needles and I feel sympathy more for him than for Reece. The man’s blood pressure and heartbeat (through meth!) are accelerated to intense levels and needles keep being inserted into different areas... the man is literally foaming at the mouth, his eyes are full of blood, and his brain is being boiled inside his skull and its like… you are slowly killing an old man with a family being left behind. But it is okay because JACK CARR says it is okay! If Jack Carr had a favorite character out of 1984, it would probably be O’Brien. Oleg and his wife and his step-kids deserved better, damnit. Random thoughts: • This is just a stupid fantasy world – how does James Reece, who has killed multiple government officials across multiple countries, get to walk around and show himself in public? The way Jack Carr writes James Reece and how people interact with him you would think there would be people lining up to gargle James Reece’s reese's pieces (if you know what I mean.) I wish Jack Carr had gotten into Dungeons and Dragons – it would have been a much healthier outlet for him. • What non-American would prefer a water with ice in it? That’s our (American) thing, Jack. • A chick who is approximately 5’5” and seems to weigh a little over 100 lbs? That chick is Very underweight. • How in the hell is James Reece meeting with the President of the USA at Camp frigging David?!!? Also, how did the President (whose name I could never remember) make it into the White House when he was unmarried? Is that even allowed in the court of public opinion? I think the one exception to the "rule" of married Presidents was James Buchanan (and he was probably gay). Yeah, I know his fiancé was murdered in a previous book, but come on. If James Reece could move on quickly (like, within weeks after Laura and Lucy being murdered), than I think the President can as well. • Katie has the personality of a fleshlight. Drink heavily and then yell himself at the mirror? • I bet the only way Reece can get his piece up is by watching Come and See or having to look up waterboarding videos. The man ain't right.

The last part of the book seemed a rushed attempt to make the storytelling format of the book come together and make sense but almost all but abandoned the original narrative format altogether. The only chapter of any interest or depth was the chapter about the character’s grandmother. This was the only chapter that had a glimpse of internal revelation or a spark of true character. At the end I thought this was akin to overhearing a banal and boring conversation between unremarkable people. And I learned little, nor grasped the immense beauty of, western Michigan from this book, sadly.

Thinner than ever, Allon disintegrates The plot of these gets thinner and thinner with every new release over the past few years. And, predictably repetitive: a beautiful woman, an espionage novice, is guided by Allon to take down the bad guys. ** spoiler alert ** Unpopular opinion ahead! (Also many, many spoilers.) A lot of purportedly intelligent people have to make a lot bizarre, nonsensical, baffling choices to get us through this storyline. How this book should have gone: 1. Lowen finds the manuscript. 2. Lowen begins to read the manuscript. 3. Weird crap starts happening in the house. 4. Lowen immediately tells Jeremy. 5. Lowen gets to one of many graphically gruesome child abuse scenes in Verity’s manuscript. 6. Lowen hands Jeremy the manuscript and says, “read this.” 7. Lowen leaves. 8. The end. How this book goes: 1. Lowen finds the manuscript. 2. Lowen begins to read the manuscript one tiny chunk at a time, spread out over weeks, so as to pad the entire length of Verity. 3. Weird crap starts happening around the house. 4. Lowen ignores the weird crap or convinces herself she made it up because…she sleep walks? (Something that was discussed so thoroughly I thought it would have a point. But it didn’t?) 5. Lowen gets to one of many graphically gruesome child abuse scenes. 6. Lowen takes a break from reading the manuscript, writes a novel (all off screen), develops sudden feelings for Jeremy, then continues to read the manuscript. 7. Lowen consents to having Jeremy lock her into her bedroom from the outside, so as to prevent her sleepwalking all over the house. 8. Lowen has unprotected sex with Jeremy with his wife and child upstairs. 9. Lowen realizes Verity killed Harper, sees Verity moving on camera, confronts her, and finally shows Jeremy the manuscript. 10. Lowen helps Jeremy kill Verity. 11. Lowen moves to North Carolina with Jeremy, where they will raise the baby with whom she is now pregnant. 12. But surprise! The whole manuscript was JUST A WRITING EXERCISE DUH 13. The end. Additional notes: The first time Lowen and Jeremy meet (yet another wildly contrived scenario including a car accident and a woman trusting a strange man to hang out in a bathroom with her while she changes her shirt) Jeremy acts like he has no idea who she is. But later says he was the one who suggested she co-author Verity’s novels because Lowen’s writing was so special to him. So he lied? Are we sure there wasn’t something more sinister going on with Jeremy? I just kept waiting for it, but it never happened. No way Verity “fakes” her condition. Doctors, nurses, specialists, all the scans and tests, and no one could tell she was just lying really still?! Excuse me. That ending is so contrived! Was I supposed to be shocked? Or upset? Because it just made me roll my eyes. I honestly feel like Colleen Hoover wrote this with the mindset that she’ll be taken more seriously as a writer the darker and more graphic her novels become. And this was the darkest and most gruesome yet. But she’s at her best when her plots are solid and don’t rely heavily on contrived “twists” and explanations and nonsensical behavior by characters. So yeah…she pushed this novel to its absolute limits, content-wise. But dropped her plotting and story telling along the way. 1 star

When you’ve almost reached the end, you have virtually all the knowledge you need….until you reach the end of said journey and find out that you didn’t need any of that knowledge to begin with. It was all for nothing, and there’s no purpose in life, and you start to question why you went on the journey in the first place, and suddenly wish you were taking a journey that’s much more worth your time than the one you just took. But it FAILS in every other category. UPDATE: I lied…book club convinced me to push it down to 1 star

I am a double vaccinated, booster-taking, non-conspiracy theorist on Covid - so my problems with this book doesn’t stem from that (unlike a few other disgruntled reviews I’ve seen). I had pre-ordered this book, as this author is one of my favorites. I throughly enjoyed her last book - Two Ways - although that one had very mixed reviews because of all the nonfiction & educational text (which I had enjoyed). I was expecting the same from this book - but with a focus on the Galapagos, evolution, and possibly Darwin’s theory. Unfortunately, this book seemed to mainly focus on Covid and all its intricacies from the beginning of March 2020 - and I do not consider this fiction as we are still living in the middle of the pandemic. It’s as if the author thought that the person reading the book hadn’t experienced the pandemic yet, and wanted to walk you through every single step of what it was like. No thank you - I read to get out of reality, not be reminded of it. I have read a successful book recently, where the author was able to use Covid as a device for two people being thrown together unexpectedly - but that’s all it was - a device. I was completely okay with that & enjoyed that story. But Covid being the focal point of a story, especially with so much of it being “informational”, is too soon. Besides that, I did not feel any connection with any of the characters - especially not the main character, who seemed selfish & self-absorbed. Unrelatable, and frankly, unrealistic. I’m not buying the complete personality makeover in a few weeks, no matter where you’ve traveled. And finally, stemming off the personality unrealism - the author makes it seem like having a Type A personality, where someone has “lists” & “plans” doesn’t get to experience life fully. As someone who is a Type A, married to someone who is Type B - we have done things both ways & still had fun. In addition to this - I see no problem with how the main character was before this - ambitious, driven, goal-oriented. Apparently this was supposed to be a character flaw that needed to be righted by the author. Overall, giving this book a one-star pained me greatly as I love this author. But I almost DNF-ed it several times - and only didn’t, because I always read through every & any book.

But she also said that it doesn’t come out of nowhere, and there were hints dropped. *looks from corner to corner of the Galapagos* girl, where? I went back and looked for hints I might’ve missed—they were not there. It was unbelievable, inauthentic, and I just felt cheated. Almost as cheated as Finn, who absolutely deserved better than the ungrateful obnoxious Diana. “You’re just not perfect for me.” …girl why? Not one of the recollections of Finn was negative or exhibited why he wasn’t good for her. Except for the fact that a doctor shouldn’t be hitting on his patients—he’s gross and he’s fired. Also, women can go three weeks without a dude. Honestly, we’ll be ok. Then there’s the portrayal of the pandemic. Even as a nurse, I eye-rolled at the melodrama of Finn’s letters and the descriptions of the pandemic. There was so much pain for frontline workers, which I saw and felt for my coworkers, and I did not feel a single thing besides annoyance while reading this. It felt absolutely forced, where every horrible detail of what happened during the pandemic was literally just written in list form and vomited on the page. I described it to a friend as a high schooler going on a first date with someone who liked Nirvana and subsequently cramming everything they read about Nirvana on Google the night prior into conversation over dinner under the guise of nonchalance. Just horrible. I’ve had discussions about whether a story like this could be done well—can half the book be a lie and still be enjoyable for the reader? I think it absolutely could. But it would require at least a hint of unreliability from the narrator, a frame that makes it believable, and a point. This book had none of those. I have nothing positive to say except that if done well, this could’ve been a masterpiece. If there was an actual setup that made it believable that she had Covid this whole time. If the pandemic was portrayed in an emotive way. If there were inconsistencies in her dream world that made you question what was actually happening. If she gave actual reasons why she should leave her fiancé other than him being a part of the plan she no longer wants, or a hot guy being available on an island. If the point of the story was what we all learned during the pandemic, which was holding what we value close, instead of discarding it like garbage. Just so disappointing. Jodi if you see this I still love you but you did us dirty.

** spoiler alert ** Daniel Silva has been one of my favorite authors for years. His books are well-written with great action, smart dialog, excellent pacing, and relevant topics. While the pacing and some of the dialog lives up to his normal standards, the rest of The Order is simply a mess. Basic idea: Gabriel and family go to Italy for vacation. Gabriel gets pulled into an investigation of the Pope's death. Gabriel and his family go home. The end. No, really. Gabriel's team make a brief cameo. Otherwise there's a lot of talking and some country hopping. No action. The crux of the story is that the Pope discovered a book written by Pontius Pilate (yes, that one) where he takes full responsibility for the death of Jesus. The Catholic Church cannot have this known since it would make the beliefs and actions of the Church, especially during WWII, wrong, and the Church cannot weather another storm like that. You might be asking yourself how a book like that could wreck the church. That's because the basis of thought here is that the persecution of the Jewish people is based on one sentence in the New Testament (Matthew 27:25 "His blood be upon us and our children.") It is certainly true the Jewish people have been persecuted over the centuries (and long before Jesus was arrested 2000 years ago). It is also true that the church and the people in it (notice I don't say Christians) have used the bible to justify horrible things. Any text can be twisted to abuse people. But no true Christian would put the blame of Jesus' death on anyone except his or her own shoulders. Jesus willingly gave up His life (Silva ignored all the verses in the bible that say that, such as John 10:18 - "No one has taken [My life] away from Me, but I lay it down on My own initiative...."). The heart of the Christian faith is that Jesus died in our place, taking our punishment for the sins we commit. The Jews didn't kill Him. Pilate and the Romans didn't kill Him. I did. But Silva goes far beyond simply blaming Christians for the persecution of the Jews and the Holocaust. He uses seemingly every liberal theological work available to say that Christianity is a false religion. While he doesn't use those words, he has several characters talk about all the reasons why the bible is wrong, one going so far as to say it's lies. Any person who claims to be a Christian but denies the divinity of Jesus or His resurrection has no idea what it means to be a Christian. He claims the gospel accounts were written decades after the fact with the intention of blaming the Jews to appease Rome and recruit Gentiles to the faith (which is ludicrous). He also claims the disciples who wrote these accounts didn't believe in the divinity of Jesus. Yet all but one died as a martyr for his beliefs. He also ignores the rest of the New Testament writings that back up the gospel claims. The most mind-boggling part is that the argument he uses to try to discredit the gospels (no original copy exists) is not taken into account for the "gospel of Pilate" that is found. It is a copy of a copy, and yet can be trusted to be the "truth." You can't have it both ways. And for some reason, Silva decided to walk into the mystical arena with Father Joshua. For those that may not be well-versed in the bible, Joshua = Yeshua. The Greek equivalent of Yeshua is Jesus. (The New Testament was written in Greek.) Father Joshua can only be seen by a few people, can seemingly walk on water, and disappears at will. Silva even has the audacity to have him speak the words of Jesus (Matthew 16:15, "Who do you say that I am?"). Even though Silva walks down this mystical path, he never once considers that perhaps evil is responsible for the persecution of God's chosen people. He also seems to think that Jesus needed to wait 2000 years to get the truth out. In the afterword, Silva expresses his desire to address the rampant anti-Semitism sweeping across the world. He is not wrong. However, there had to have been a better way to do this than to attack Christianity. He even acknowledges that those who believe in the inerrancy of the bible, of whom I am one, will be offended by this work (I am). He definitely had an agenda to pound here. I hope it was worth it to him. He has lost this reader.

I am stunned to see that this book has a rating of 4 1/2 stars. I can't believe how bad it is. Anything Dan Brown can do, Silva will do worse. And I loathed Dan Brown's books. This is a dull, tedious diatribe that is so unbelievable and unconvincing.

Silva desperately wants to be Dan Brown. His hatred of Christians, and the Catholic Church in particular, are tiresome.
