1.25/5 ⭐ Now I will say this because I know it needs to be said: I don't blame or throw hate towards the people who enjoyed this and got carried away with the whole thing. Good etiquette says that we don't discuss tastes and colours (de gustibus, you know). Each on their own. I'm just analysing the information that I have received from this book and get to conclusions that are strictly personal. Yes, I do know everyone is crazy about this. No, I do not understand why. Yes, I did award it with a little star. No, I'm not taking it back. No take backsies here. I mean, listen. Objectively speaking, I could see exactly where everyone was balling their eyes out because the scenes were made to be emotional and heartbreaking and to devastate the hearts of those who were caught up in the story...but I wasn't caught up in it. I was in complete sanity, actually. I rarely give this kind of low rating to books. Usually, when I simply can't enjoy a novel for various reasons, I'll award it 2 stars. When I get to the point where I give a book 1 star then this is a case of personal offence. Yes, I've been offended by this book and I'll tell you why: because it is marketed as a romance and this is not the kind of romance we should be giving away freely like that. As an adult in a healthy relationship, this kind of thing might not affect me or you or anyone else that's in a good place, emotionally and romantically speaking. But because of this wrongly placed kind of advertisement, people (and especially teenagers) with not so much experience in this field or with a darker experience than it was fair of them to have, might think that this is how ravishing love should look like and, unconsciously, they wish for that in their lives (maybe, even though I hope not). They might relate to this and not have the right capacities to discern between what's good and what's toxic for them. Not everyone understands the meaning of something when it's disguised. I couldn't see how and why the MCs fell in love. It simply didn't get to me. But honestly I'm more of a slow-burn kind of romance lover at this point. They meet and make up a little excuse of a game of 'naked truths' where they say the stupidest things they were thinking or the stupidest things they have ever done in their lives. They don't see each other anymore for a long time. They meet again and fall in love so much their skulls crack and they stopped thinking logically. From there, everything in their relationship moves with a speed not even the newest model of Ferrari can keep up with. I don't buy this, sorry. On top of that, their whole relationship (yeah I know, dreamy as it is, shocking!) it's very toxic. The fact that Lily Bloom (yes, that's her name for real) grew up in an abusive household and that her mum decided to bury the abuse, treating it like nothing, is one thing. But when Lily gets to be in a similar kind of relationship with Ryle, there's a whole other story. The worst part is that she realises that Ryle's anger issues are similar to her father's, but she (like her mum did) tries to convince herself it's nothing. I know it's hard to break free, so damn hard. But how can you love an abuser so deeply after only 200 pages? Time lapses included. The relationship is toxic. Ryle is abusive and manipulative. Oh but wait. He is a walking and talking romance novel cliche lover: handsome, hot, rich, one of the youngest neurosurgeons you'd ever meet in your life, wow yeah that man is sex-on-a-stick, seriously I'm shocked I wasn't expecting all this perfection, give me a lifetime to recover. He is an abusive man, in all his hot perfection, and the fact that abuse comes on the house is terrible enough but the way this whole abuse theme of the book is treated and overlooked makes me want to believe that even though romance is supposed to be the main theme here, it has been built on abuse in a manner that should've been emotional. However, the poor execution of the theme exposure and expansion made romance the lame excuse of the actual main theme of the book: overlooked abuse. Abuse is a complex thing to write about. It's not a joke and it shouldn't be treated superficially. Yes, I know in the end the title makes sense because Lily Bloom is now as strong as a mamma bear protecting her little cubs from the Evil (trademark), but this only happens in the last few pages. This is not heartbreaking, this is just sad and a little bit infuriating that the book takes toxic relationships and makes them look like romance. And it's unfair that teenage girls believe this is what love should look like. My lovely girls, love has a force of its own that no one can explain but it doesn't bring you pain. It doesn't hurt you, it doesn't mess up with you, and it doesn't make you question yourself. Love makes your heart feel fuzzy and warm like it's too big and too alive for your chest to hold it in anymore. Makes you turn red and shy and teary-eyed because you felt touched and seen in more ways than physically. It gives you the prophetic wings to fly. It makes you want to be better, safer, warmer, happier and healthier. Love is not about the money, the riches, the fame, the handsomeness and hotness. It is about that someone or those someones that make you feel invincible. In love, we only suffer from too many warm feelings, not from abuse, toxicity and cringe. Because let me tell you, this book is all about these latter three ones, when in fact it should've been about all those others I've said before. My honest opinion here comes to a conclusion: the excuse of abuse to build love became actually an overlooked and under-talked-about leitmotif of a book that could've easily been more balanced if, instead of giving Lily Bloom even the idea of a life with Ryle, the author would've just told the story of her first love directly as it was, and not through the childish excuse of a young Lily writing diary-style letters to Ellen DeGeneres and talking about her first love. Their love story was closer to what romance should look like and (bonus!!!) it was also tragic enough (for the drama lovers out there).

(edit: seeing the book go from 4.4 stars to 4.26 i believe makes me extra happy inside)

Colleen Hoover’s It Ends With Us is a book my wife absolutely loves. I watched the movie with her a while ago and didn't enjoy it. My wife insisted I read the book. Sorry to say, I liked it even less. The book explores why women who experience domestic violence find it so difficult to leave. The premise is good, and the reasons make sense. I completely understand why these women might stay with a man who hurts them, whether it's because they still love him, have hope for the relationship, or fear the unknown of leaving. But I haven't read something so physically repulsive in a long time. Lily is a complete weakling, both mentally and physically. The most powerful weapon of the weak is words, and in this novel, verbal abuse is very common. But doesn't emotional abuse count as abuse? Isn't verbal abuse also a form of abuse? Or is it overlooked because both the author and the protagonist are women? Here are a few reasons why I dislike Lily: Firstly, Ryle, physically abused Lily 3 times, and I only empathized with her on the last occasion. The first time, Ryle and Lily were drunk, Ryle went to grab a plate, only to hurt his hands. While Ryle was in pain, Lily didn't react but laughed. Then, she jokingly leaned in to look at Ryle's hand. Ryle pushed her, sending her to the ground. First of all, Ryle didn't do it on purpose. Secondly, he was in physical pain and acted impulsively. When Lily fell to the ground, she was completely dumbfounded and kept crying, asking why Ryle would push her and caused pain to her head. She didn't care at all that Ryle had a major surgery the next day and that his hands were crucial to his future career. So, Lily's attitude was basically, as long as you love me, that's all that matters. I don't care about your personal development or anything else. In the end, it was Ryle who repeatedly apologized before Lily forgave him. The second time, Ryle found Lily's ex - boyfriend's phone number hidden behind her phone and got angry. The reason was that after the first incident when Lily was injured, her ex saw her and got into a fight with Ryle, so Ryle was very jealous. He smashed Lily's phone and stormed out. Lily ran after him, but during the struggle, she was pushed down the stairs. This time, I kind of understood, but wasn’t it still unintentional? This doesn't align with my understanding of domestic violence. Shouldn't domestic violence be when someone intentionally wants to hurt you to vent their anger? Ryle didn't want to hurt her or 'punish' her by pushing her. But the harm was done. This time, Lily cried even harder, saying she was even more scared, and repeatedly compared Ryle to her abusive father. Ryle continued to apologize and coax her, but he didn't get Lily's sincere forgiveness. Lily kept accusing and suspecting that Ryle would continue to hurt her. The third time: Ryle bought Lily a house. After moving in, Ryle found Lily's diary and discovered that the fridge magnet she always carried was a gift from her ex, who even named his restaurant after her. Additionally, Lily has a tattoo on her neck that was also for him. Coincidentally, Ryle used to really like that tattoo, and that spot had become a point of intimacy for them. When Ryle waited for Lily to come home, he asked her where the fridge magnet came from. Lily lied and said she bought it a long time ago. Then Ryle, in a fit of anger, bit Lily's neck, making her bleed, and dragged her to the bed to forcefully engage in intimacy, but he eventually stopped. That night, Lily was extremely disappointed in Ryle and secretly called her ex to come pick her up and leave. In these 3 incidents, which 1 wasn't caused by Lily's lack of transparency in their relationship resulting in misunderstandings? They never had a proper conversation about their past before getting married, so after the second incident, shouldn't they have been open and talked about their histories? Honestly, I think Ryle's jealousy and anger are normal reactions. What is not acceptable is the intentional harm he caused in the last incident, which was indeed wrong. However, I really don't believe that the mishaps in the first 2 instances can be categorized as domestic violence. Secondly, Lily is just someone who can only whine and complain. In both instances, she was pushed down and got hurt. She's really quite fragile. What kind of person suffers a concussion after being pushed down? Furthermore, she is an adult, not a disabled or a child. When you are harmed, I would much rather see her fight back than get hurt and then whimper and cry about how his man treated her poorly. I want her to get up after being pushed and slap him across the face, then look him in the eyes and tell him that if he touches her again, she will fight back. I understand that women naturally have less muscle strength than men, but I want to see resistance; I want to see her speak up for herself in the moment. Explain, and justify. It's frustrating to see her getting hurt, and then later, when Ryle has regained his composure and is continuously apologizing, they exchange power and Lily uses sarcasm and emotional abuse against Ryle. It should be a balanced reaction in the heat of the moment, rather than her waiting until he has calmed down to retaliate after being harmed. Thirdly, Lily is quite adept at playing victim tactics herself. She clings to her feelings for her ex. Just ask, who can tell their spouse after getting married: ”I will always love my first love”? She dares to do it, and she does so very naturally. In a confrontation with Ryle later on, she tells him she’ll always loved her first love, Ryle can't stop her, and Ryle can't hurt her for it. Even if Ryle catch her with him in bed today, Ryle can't lay a finger on her. Wow, that’s a b*tch for saying such piercing words. I feel like Lily is not the brave female figure that Colleen Hoover tries to portray; she is nowhere near bravery. She gives me the impression of an assassin - stabbing when you least expect it. But when you confront her face-to-face, she doesn't dare to say a word. I would have admired her even more if she had slapped back once when she was harmed. Unfortunately, she didn't. It's fortunate that she lives in the U.S., as if she were here in my country, she probably wouldn’t understand our definition of "justifiable defense." Immediate retaliation is what is called justifiable defense, while going back later to strike is considered intentional harm. In short, after finishing this book, I felt utterly speechless. I thought I would see a story about equality or female empowerment, but instead, I saw a female assassin standing on a moral high ground, incessantly saying, "You can't hurt me, you can't touch me, and I can spit on you from behind. But as long as you touch me, you must be submissive to me for the rest of your life, It's not because I have power, but because I stand on moral high ground and because I am a woman, I am weak, and I am justified.” Really? Finally, I want to talk about some plot points that have nothing to do with the main storyline but really disgusted me: 1. According to the description, Ryle is a doctor, wealthy, handsome, and has no shortage of sexual partners. Yet, he falls in love with Lily after just a twenty-minute conversation on a rooftop and even kneels down to beg her for a one-night stand. Does Colleen Hoover look down on doctors or handsome guys? And initially, Ryle said he wasn't interested in a stable relationship. But after meeting Lily, he immediately starts a relationship, gets married after 6 months, and then wants a child right away. Is Lily a witch or something? 2. According to the description, Lily has no friends. She had some in college, but for some reason, she doesn't have many friends after she starts working. As a result, whenever she needs someone, she always turns to her ex. Question: If Lily is as perfect as Ryle describes, why doesn't she have a new boyfriend after so long or even a close friend? 3. Besides Ryle and Lily, all the other characters are just tools to support Lily. Ryle's sister is incredibly wealthy but loves Lily and is her best friend. When she finds out that her brother has a conflict with Lily, her first reaction is to tell Lily to leave him. No one who has lived with their siblings for over a decade would change their stance so quickly. Ryle's parents are devout Christians. Even before meeting Lily, they expressed a special fondness for her. When they met for the first time, Lily was nervous and joked to Ryle, 'Your parents won't think I'm a godless whore for living with you before we're married, will they?' And his parents overheard and casually said, 'You're not a whore; you're the best girl I've ever met.' Can you feel the disgust I felt when I read that part? I’m sorry but Colleen Hoover clearly doesn't understand the concept of cultural differences and respect. She thinks she can gloss over anything with American-style casualness and cheerfulness, huh? 4. Ryle often appears in front of Lily wearing a surgical gown. Lily finds it very sexy. I don't know which hospital would allow doctors to wear surgical gowns outside of the operating room. And at the end, Ryle goes to work wearing a surgical gown. So, surgical gowns are like uniforms now? 5. Everything comes too easily for Lily. It's just a wish - fulfillment fantasy, without any real-world relevance. She wants to open a flower shop, and poof, it's open. Her flower shop is a departure from traditional femininity, using more black to express boldness and rebellion. I don't know why her flower shop is so successful, but it just is. Ryle is rich and buys her a house whenever she wants. He has his dream job, but Lily says no, and he quits. Her high school sweetheart joins the military after graduation, opens a restaurant after his service, and it's incredibly successful. At the end, her ex’s restaurant has branches. Ryle's sister, who is also Lily's best friend, is wealthy but loves to work for Lily. When Lily is pregnant, her sister gives her a lot of unworn designer maternity clothes. When Lily is feeling down, her sister helps her manage the shop so she doesn't have to worry. Basically, everyone who likes Lily is rich, and she never lacks money. Like, are you insulting my intelligence? I am a rational person, perhaps rational people can't truly enjoy Collen Hoover’s novels. But isn't logic supposed to triumph over emotion? I’m sorry. 0.1 / 5 stars

1.5 It’s been months and I still can’t stop thinking about this book and how much its existence & popularity enrages me, so maybe I will write a review soon idk

Why Katabasis Doesn’t Work R.F. Kuang has built her reputation on sharp, ambitious novels that interrogate power, colonialism, and language in a way that felt both urgent and original. Even when polarizing, her books usually succeeded in provoking meaningful conversation. Katabasis, however, represents a startling departure, not because it risks too much, but because it risks so little. It takes a premise as rich as a descent into hell and reduces it to a derivative, unimaginative, and often offensive narrative. The significance of this failure lies not only in the book itself, but in what it suggests about Kuang’s trajectory as a writer. 1. Trigger Warnings and the Ethics of Shock Value One of the first and most glaring issues is the book’s reliance on disturbing content for impact. Katabasis introduces a long catalogue of triggers: animal cruelty, eating disorders (“She was very proud of the days that she forgot to eat…”), child murder, bestiality, gore, sexism, suicidal ideation, lesbophobia, gaslighting, sexual harassment, and more. Yet none of these are thematically developed or given narrative weight. They are presented, often graphically, then abandoned. This reliance on shock value reveals a fundamental problem: the book confuses provocation with depth. Literature can and should engage with disturbing realities, but it carries an ethical responsibility to do so with purpose and care. In Katabasis, these moments function as spectacle, not critique. Instead of illuminating power, suffering, or ideology, they leave the reader with revulsion and little else. 2. Alice and the Repetition of Misogyny Alice, the protagonist, epitomizes another failure. Her voice is abrasive not in a complex, thought-provoking way, but in a manner that forecloses engagement. She sneers at activists, despises other women, and reduces feminism to caricature. The most telling lines “She couldn’t stand those screeching activists who believed the only politically just thing was to become a lesbian.” “The best way to prove women were not inferior was just to not be inferior.” read less like the construction of a flawed but challenging character than as unexamined ventriloquism of misogynistic discourse. This is particularly troubling because it repeats a pattern across Kuang’s oeuvre. Her women often emerge as hostile to solidarity, dismissive of activism, or antagonistic toward their own sex. In Babel, such a stance was contextually coherent and ultimately challenged. In Katabasis, it is neither contextualized nor interrogated. The misogyny stands largely unopposed, raising the question: why is this the third time in four books that Kuang has turned to this trope? When repetition becomes pattern, it begins to reflect less on the characters and more on the limits of the authorial imagination. 3. The Failure of Setting and World-Building A descent into hell carries immense literary and cultural weight. From Dante to Milton, from Bosch’s paintings to modern fantasy, hell is a space where theology, morality, and imagination converge. In Katabasis, however, hell is disappointingly superficial. What should be a site of philosophical, religious, and mythological resonance is reduced to aesthetic borrowing: Dantean gestures, Mad Max wastelands, The Atlas Six-style magic. The absence of religious or cultural depth suggests not only a lack of research, but also a missed opportunity. Hell, by definition, is never neutral; it is always entangled with systems of belief, punishment, and morality. To strip it of this context is to render it generic. Compared with works like The Saint of Heartbreak, which explores hell with nuance and empathy even when it is not the central subject, Kuang’s hell appears unimaginative, less a site of meaning than a backdrop for spectacle. 4. Romance and Character Flatness The marketing of Katabasis as a romance further compounds its failure. Peter is inert, his twist neither surprising nor transformative. The relationship between him and Alice lacks chemistry, intimacy, or narrative function. What emerges is not romance but proximity dressed up as intimacy. More than that, it exposes a weakness in Kuang’s writing: she has never attempted romance seriously before, and here it becomes clear why. The novel confuses proximity with connection and marketability with emotional investment. More broadly, the novel suffers from a collapse of subtle characterization. We are told Alice seeks validation. We are told she believes herself superior. We are told how to read her. What is lost is ambiguity, space for interpretation, or the beauty of implication. Where Babel married lush prose with complexity, Katabasis settles for blunt exposition. 5. The Problem of Audience The most revealing flaw in Katabasis is its lack of a clear target audience. The novel gestures toward many readerships but satisfies none: - Too academic for a general audience, yet too shallow for readers steeped in classical or theological traditions. - Marketed as a romance, yet offering none of the emotional or narrative beats of the genre. - Potentially aimed at fans of hell-as-setting, yet doing nothing new. This tension reveals something about Kuang herself. She has often drawn heavily from her academic training, with Babel showcasing the brilliance and the pitfalls of this approach. In Katabasis, however, the academic bubble becomes a cage. The novel reads as though designed more to demonstrate cleverness than to engage readers. The result is a work that is alienating without being profound, academic without being rigorous. 6. Resolution Without Consequence If the novel spends much of its time mired in provocation, its ending swings abruptly in the opposite direction. The resolution is oddly simplistic, almost Disney-like in its moral clarity: the “good” prevail, the “bad” are punished, and the toxicity of academia is neutralized. This undercuts whatever complexity the novel might have gestured toward. For a writer known for her refusal to offer easy answers, this tidy resolution feels especially hollow. 7. The Larger Question: Why Does This Book Exist? All of this leads to the central problem: Katabasis lacks purpose. It does not advance mythology in new directions, it does not interrogate misogyny with depth, it does not develop its disturbing content into critique. It offers spectacle, but not meaning. For an author who has positioned herself as a serious voice in contemporary literature, interrogating empire in The Poppy War and language in Babel, this regression is striking. Katabasis is not ambitious, but hollow; not daring, but careless. What once distinguished Kuang was her ability to weave influences into something distinctively her own. If earlier works invited readers to wrestle with questions of power and morality, this one invites little more than frustration and disgust. What does it mean for an author like Kuang to fail in this way? Katabasis is not a failure of excess, but of imagination. It does not stumble because it reaches too far, but because it settles for the shallow, the derivative, the shock-for-shock’s-sake. As part of Kuang’s trajectory, this matters. She has established herself as a writer who interrogates systems of power, empire, language, colonialism, academia. But with Katabasis, the critique collapses. Misogyny is repeated but not interrogated. Disturbing content is invoked but not explored. Hell is evoked but not imagined. Even academia, the one theme Kuang does know intimately, is reduced at the end to a simplistic moral victory. The result is not simply a disappointing novel. It is a work that calls into question whether Kuang, at this stage in her career, has become trapped within her own academic frame of reference, mistaking spectacle for substance and provocation for profundity. If The Poppy War was her confrontation with empire, and Babel her meditation on language, then Katabasis should have been her descent into the underworld of meaning itself. Instead, it is an empty masquerade, a regression that leaves wondering whether the author who once dared to unsettle us has been replaced by someone content to imitate herself.

«Katabasis» is a paradox: a book so desperate to be profound that it ends up saying absolutely nothing. Every page strains under the weight of its own cleverness, as though style alone could substitute for substance. Spoiler—it can’t. The prose is mock-academic to the point of parody, endlessly circling itself in self-referential loops. Characters are less people than megaphones, shouting Kuang’s arguments until the whole thing collapses into a lecture dressed up as a novel. The narrative, meanwhile, trudges along like it knows it doesn’t matter. Reading this was like watching someone congratulate themselves in real time—dense where it should be deep, smug where it should be smart. Kuang confuses paradox for brilliance, but the only true paradox here is how something so overwritten can feel so empty. One star, because even pretension should at least be entertaining.

While reading this book, I thought about RF Kuang. Not as a person, as an entity, a presence, a space in the wider conversation about books, genre, the concept of "having things to say", and so on. That's primarily because the book was too dull to provide engaging thought on its own, but I digress. This is the second of her big releases to focus on academia, and The Poppy War had a fair emphasis on it as well. Kuang has been a student for a long time. This seems only natural that she would have things to say about it, and while Babel was far from my favorite book, it had a point of view, things to say, concepts to engage with, prose that occasionally felt poignant, some measure of intrigue and stakes. So why does Katabasis regress in every category? Both of these books -strike that, all Kuang books- are filled with rage. That is the through line of every word of prose Rebecca Kuang has ever written - rage. But there comes a point during any outburst of rage where you no longer have new thoughts about the reason you're angry, you are merely working yourself up over and over again in different ways because your nervous system is not ready to relax, not ready to let it go, your rage has not been satiated or validated yet and so you grasp onto that famous phrase of the angry - "AND ANOTHER THING....!!!". Eventually, someone hands us a cup of tea or a beer or gives us a hug and says, "yeah, that really sucks". And you no longer need to tell us about another thing. Katabasis is the literary "and another thing" that has no clue what it wants to say, no coherence or cohesion, just blindly throwing things into the narrative with no larger picture of how to integrate these concepts. Rebecca. It really sucks that academia is so toxic. It sounds hard, and unfair, and sexist, and racist, and no doubt you and many other graduate students feel a giant ball of injustice in your chest when you think about how unfair it all is. Now can we move on? Katabasis is a metaphor made literal, with no shocking twists, no interesting concepts, no rehashing of older ideas in new ways, no compelling characters, no satisfying arcs or exciting challenges or really much of anything besides "academia is hell". Literally. And it occasionally tries to grapple with interesting topics, things that for just a brief moment, I was like, "oh, this new idea will be interesting", only for it to consistently choose the least interesting route. Alice, our MC, is one of the most insufferable characters I've ever read about, yet I am never left with an impression that I am supposed to dislike her, merely scold her gently for her worst traits (like hating those sluts who had the audacity to sleep with their professor, whom she also wants to sleep with) and accept that while rough around the edges, her goal of entering hell to rescue her professor so he can write a recommendation letter is a noble one. But don't worry, folks! Alice will be humbled by nice guy Peter. Humbled, simply by being in his presence for a large portion of this book and them both talking about random bits of philosophy or history or literature just to show that Kuang has read them too. There is no tangent too tangential to be included in "and another thing", that's what another things are for. This "enemies to lovers" is only that on the strictest, more bare bones of technicalities, mostly these two are together for them to both randomly get character growth without earning it. Another review said the book has "proximity dressed up as intimacy" and I have never felt a slam dunk of a line so keenly. These two bond and change simply because the narrative demands it. The book is first person from Alice's POV and reveals backstory in almost "throwing a dart"-esque fashion. There are so many valid ways to reveal information throughout a story, but the narrator telling you a small sliver of the story and then 200 pages later going, in internal monologue, "actually here's the rest of the story" which paints everything in a new light is boring and artificial. This isn't presented in an unreliable narrator type of way, simply "we are now a third of the way through the book, so I need to reveal more information about Alice's relationship with the professor" is so evidently the driving force that I was annoyed every time. This is not how internal monologues work. If I am thinking about why I like or dislike a person, I think about the biggest reasons in either direction first. I don't think "yeah, George is kinda shitty, he left my text on read last week" and then three days later remember, "oh yeah, George also shot my brother in the face". Also this book has lembas bread. Actually. Lembas bread, from Lord of the Fucking Rings. They go to hell, and say, man I'm hungry, lets eat some lembas. What in the absolute fuck is going on here. Did Kuang write the whole book, realize they never ate something, have two days to fix this in revision, and go, "screw it, lembas bread, as made famous in Lord of the Rings by most famous fantasy author of all time, JRR Tolkien, that will be fine"? This is absurd and I'm so infuriated that nobody else is talking about this. I demand an explanation. The resolution of this book is trite and unearned, every level of hell is the most boring, easy, uninteresting route. There is a cat, though, so not everything is pure misery. You could just watch the movie Whiplash instead of reading this book. Whiplash is two hours and handles the questions, "how far does one go in pursuit of being the absolute best, what do they give up to do so? How far does one go as an educator to coax greatness out of someone, how far is too far? What should be the consequences of either of these decisions?" much better than this book does and you might even feel some stakes or sense of tension in that movie instead of being lulled into your slow death by the 80th reference to Dante or Borges or Hume or whatever. AND ANOTHER THING!! The lembas bread!! You couldn't have just renamed it!? Keep it the same but call it Kuang Bread? Rage Bread? Sabmel bread? Anything!! 2/10

I am genuinely mystified at the amount of praise this book is getting. I found the novel practically unreadable. For starters I am totally allergic to fiction written in the present tense but even apart from that it comes across as total fan wankery. Then again no wonder given it was written by Tarantino's biggest fan. Hell, he even featured references to himself as a young boy in it! Don't even know where to start so just a few small pointers why I reacted so strongly about this one. I certainly wasn't "nodding in the affirmative" (one of Tarantino's many awkward expressions in the book) when I was reading this The book forsakes plot nearly entirely and just feels like listening in to some geeky bores ramble on and on and on and on about their favourite movies. It's bad enough when this is about real movies but gets worse when we read (but obviously can't see) references to fictitious films. And worst of all, Tarantino, self nominated movie geek Numero Uno, often gets his facts wrong about the real stuff: The "info" about the German Karl May movies e.g. has glaring errors starting from apparently having been filmed in the 1950s when they didn't get made until a decade later. Every character regardless defines themselves by their love of movies and apparently Cliff even goes as far as having a Top 5 list of favourite Kurosawa films. Defining characters by their interests is always a very lazy way of characterisation but en masse such as here it is total ineptitude. (If he ever gets around to writing a Western novel as threatened I have no doubt it will mainly be an endless list of favourite Lily Langtry songs.) So he drops the film's raison d'être and (in)famous climax by making this just a tiny reference point very early on in the novel. This of course means that all the scenes with the Manson Family lose any of their meaning but hey, I am willing to forgive that if he had replaced the ending with something new and worthwhile. Instead the final pages are - no spoiler warning as there is nothing of interest to spoil - an endless reading of lines between Rick and the Girl???? Seriously??!!!??? That's it????? Events in the future (1970s) are told in the conditional or past tense; events in the present (1969) in present tense; events in the past in past tense so with both past and future in the past tense, it's a stylistic nightmare together with the awkward present tense narration. "If New York is the city that never sleeps, Los Angeles in the middle of the night and early wee hours of the morning turns back into the desert it was before it got paved over with concrete." Sorry, but this kind of stuff hurts both my eyes and ears when I read it aloud and every page if not every paragraph is choke full of dreadful narration. I could go on endlessly (as Tarantino does) but I've had it with this book. Given that Tarantino is known for films with snappy and succinct dialogue, I am utterly stunned how clumsy this is written in every aspect.

I love QT, and OUATI HOLLYWOOD was my favorite film of 2019, but this is not a novel, or even a novelization, it's an assemblage of supplemental written material undoubtedly developed over a period of some years before QT actually made the movie. Some chapters read like early-draft screenwriting, some like prose backstory for the eventual cast, and some could be QT walking around spitballing ideas into a tape recorder. All of which are admirable techniques a master storyteller might use to develop an original work, but having your staff then cobble all these loose bits into a paperback and selling it as a "novel" is disingenuous. Every scene or idea he eventually used in the movie is palpably, embarrassingly worse here. Nor will this book "enhance your experience of the movie," any more than six hours of re-cut Beatles footage enhanced LET IT BE, or that visit to the French rubber plantation enhanced APOCALYPSE NOW.

I really wish I could react as positively to this book as most of the reviews I've seen for it are on here. Right, strong opinion out of the way, better do some actual reviewing. Spoilers btw. We start in Groundhog Day, but no, not a funny, entertaining, charming one with Bill Murray, with the nob-King Gavilar dying AGAIN. This time the action is through Navani's eyes. She sees her husband talk with people we now know to be Heralds, weird spheres are mentioned. The main thing is Gavilar insults her and tells her she isn't a scholar, and man do we have to hear about that a lot in the 1200 pages to come. Part 1 Kaladin is depressed. We know this because it's all he talks and thinks about. I'm not saying that's not what depressed people do and I'm not saying that it isn't hard. But why am I reading about it? It's just boring. He also does a bit of flying about and there's a battle for Hearthstone. The action sequences with the use of Surges explained in horrifying detail just leave me detached and unexcited at this point. They use it to evacuate the people of Hearthstone whilst the Fused version of Windrunners attack them. They find a great strategist general from Herdaz who the audiobook actor (who was terrible throughout) for some reason made into a bad approximation of South African. Someone phones up Navani and says imprisoning spren isn't cool. Kaladin meets Leshwi who he likes and the Pursuer he doesn't. He kills the Pursuer who from then on wants to (you guess it) pursue him. This starts his journey to become one of the worst and ineffectual villains of all time. Kaladin also meets Moash, who wants him to kill himself, which isn't very nice. Dalinar tells him he can't fight anymore due to massive PTSD. Shallan is unfortunately still alive and is being Shallan (or Veil, or Radiant) in the Warcamps. She's trying to infiltrate the Sons of Honor who are apparently still knocking about led by Sadeas' wife Ialai. Someone kills Ialai and it's obviously one of Shallan's personalities but that's apparently a secret that we have to wait 1o00 pages to be revealed. She continues with monologues along the lines of "no one would like the real me" and is generally annoying and pathetic. Venli is in Kholinar and is serving Leshwi. We see a bit of Fused society. It's sort of weird. Raboniel is introduced as a crazy scientist who nearly killed everyone once with a bioweapon. So, most of the humans get back to Urithuru about half way through Part 1. The bigwigs have a meeting and discuss where the war effort's gonna go. They decide on Emul. Dalinar and Jasnah are going to go. More plot is needed for the others so the concept of a lack of Radiants is introduced. Adolin and Shallan are sent to Shadesmar (joy of joys) to convince the Honorspren to form more bonds. This works for Shallan who has been told by Mraize (her Ghostblood handler) to go there take down Sons of Honor leader Restares. The world's worst fellowship is assembled, Adolin and Dalinar have some awkward dialogue, and off they go. Also Shallan thinks one of them is a spy even though we all know she killed Ialai and no one is a spy. The Fused decide to attack Urithiru at Raboniel's prompting. Kaladin talks to Zahel and a Cosmere exposition dump happens regarding Investiture. I don't know why he doesn't just update the Coppermind wiki himself and not pretend the infodump is happening due to a real organic conversation. Part 2 to 5 (I forgot to keep sectioning them off) So then it happens and just like that, with a lot of their forces half-way across Roshar, the Fused just take Urithiru, reverse the defences of the Tower and reduce most of the Radiants defending it to comatose, dribbling messes. It's almost as if, if you knew someone was a spy for the enemy you shouldn't keep him appraised of your exact battleplans! Genius from Dalinar as usual. Cue 100s of pages of Navani locked in some kind of fantasy science war with Raboniel (who I think was my favourite character, mostly by virtue of hating her the least). They strike up a mutual respect, work together, create "The Rhythm of War" (ah there it is, the title of the book) which is both a combination of the rhythms of Honor and Odium and a notebook. In this time many, many ridiculous things are said, like "the song of science itself" and it gets very pseudotechnical. There is page after page of explanation of how the tones of Roshar match the light of the respective Shards, and fabrial-science, and I think at one point a weird analogy of the real physical properties of Wave Interference and matter / antimatter annihilation. It was like Sanderson had been reading GCSE (or American equivalent) physics textbooks and just threw a few things in and wrapped it up in his worldbuilding. I have no idea why. If anyone enjoyed any of that, well, they're bigger Cosmere fans than me. Just writing that has made me recall the horror-filled minutes of my life listening to the explanation for the lateral and vertical movements of the bloody platform via fabrology that nobody asked for. If you do the thinking in the background as an author to make sure something is technically possible within the constructs of your own invented world and underlying physical principles, then fine, great due diligence; just don't subject us poor readers of your book to it! There is an underlying theme here, which is that this book was massively under-edited. There's no way it needed to be as long as it is. Authors should never stop listening to their editor and I'm not having that they were happy with 100% of the content of this absolute tome. Anyway all of this comes to an end (I can't call it conclusion, climax, or crescendo as it had the feel of none of them) when Navani bonds with the Sibling, the spren at the heart of the Tower. The Sibling is massively annoying throughout, it and Navani (who consistently STILL doesn't see anyone's point when they say the lighteyes and people like her were terrible to the underclasses when in power and is just pretty much still a bigot) deserve each other. This magically reverses everything that went wrong (oh, also she kills Raboniel by betraying the fuck out of her) with the Tower and just as quickly as it went wrong, everything is OK again in Urithuru. Terrible jeopardy, terrible action, terrible end. As all of that was going on, Kaladin was awake at the tower, just not doing a lot really. A lot of filler stuff. Towards the end he kills the Pursuer again fairly easily and saves his Dad, who had been a prick to him for most of the book. At some point prior to this his time was slowed down by Dalinar and the Stormfather and he said the fourth ideal and I dunno, got more powerful or something or at least got his powers back enough to not die when falling from the tower. He accepts that there's people he can't save. Great. I'd be good at that one. Meanwhile in Shadesmar Adolin is put on trial. I hated Adolin the least in this book, probably because I was too busy hating everyone else, and one of his trial chapters I enjoyed. His sword speaks up for him. The incredibly annoying Honorspen are put in their place a bit and they generally convince a few more spren to bond humans. Hurray. Shallan has inner turmoil and realisations about herself which are INCREDIBLY similar to ones she's already made. She is unVeiled. Ahhhh. Radiant's still there though, so god knows what we're gonna have to go through to get rid of her. Shallan leaves the ghostbloods for good having spoken with Restares, who is actually Kelek, a very indecisive herald who wants off of Roshar. Which most people, including me, seem to also want. Venli, Rlain and I think Leshwi do some things in the tower with the Fused and singer populations and convince a few of them to turn sides. To be honest my concentration was waning madly at that point and the dozens of Eshonai / Venli flashback chapters had trained my brain to totally switch off anytime a Singer / Listener was the POV. Not really sure what happened but I think Rlain got a spren and Venli ended up with the surviving listeners and her mum and I don't care. Taravangian is a jibbering idiot throughout most of the book and nothing really happens around him. Then he becomes Odium. Just like that. He arranges a situation with Szeth (who I used to like but now not so much), Nightblood and Rays (Odium's person thing) and yeah, becomes a God Shard thing. Probably the most interesting part of the whole book is the very end, where Taravangian has some thoughts as the new Odium while talking to Cultivation and also when he appears to Hoid and steals some of his memories. That actually had intrigue and it was the last bit of the whole 1200 pages. Urghhh. Jasnah isn't really in it at all, which is a shame because I like her character. She manipulates a bloke into allowing her to exile him, she talks about how she prefers thinking with people to sex which was a bit odd and tries out military leadership for absolutely no reason. She also advances theories on government which are getting closer to modern democracies. Oh yeah, Teft dies. That should have had more impact, but I didn't care by that point. It's too long and too rubbish. Overall I'd say nothing consequential occurs in this whole book, except the very end which is done in about five minutes. There is way, WAY too much discussion of mental health. I don't know if this is cynical point-scoring by the author as so many people have mental health issues and this is an easy way to get them on-side or whether he's one of those terrible genuine people who want to discuss it but I've totally had enough of it. Depression, anxiety, PTSD, split personality disorder, crabs, addiction, this book has it all. Well, maybe not crabs. And that might not be a mental health issue. But God, do we need to hear about it this much? I get that people have these issues, but I don't know about anyone else, but I read fantasy for escapism, not to hear more about how rubbish someone is feeling. I could get that by talking to my family. It is boring. Really boring. And kind of a downer. That's just my opinion. Nothing happens. Way too long. The pacing is way off. The characters are almost all completely annoying. Mental health issues everywhere. Barely any jeopardy. Absolutely no climax. I don't care. Hoid is still interesting.

At this point, Davenport is so boring and defanged, he might as well be the dad in a sitcom. With all his rough edges sanded down, this character has never been less interesting. Long gone are the days of interesting villains. Decades after reading them, I can still recall the baddies from Eyes of Prey, Winter, night and Mind Prey. Absolutely terrifying and remorseless, those guys were some of the scariest and most interesting put to page. For the life of me, I can't remember the last bad guy that made any kind of impression on me; instead, for decades, we've been given a group antagonists as boring and interchangeable as this series book titles. Back in Davenport's salad days, his coworkers and friends and fellow cops were as a whole, the second most appealing aspect of the classic Prey novels. Those friendships and relationships provided some of the highlights of this series. They were funny when they wanted to be and provided real drama when put in danger. I get that Lucas has changed jobs a few times over since the good old days and that someone doesn't work with or stay in touch with even the closest friends but no one of much noticed has ever stepped in to replace the old crew. Lately we have had Bob and Rae but Sandford doesn't seem interested in the least of filling out either character. In fact, here in Ocean Prey, we get hints that he's already growing tired of them. Plot-wise, we get one or two action scenes propping up pages and pages of the good guys not doing much of anything. And then they have meetings and admit to each other they still have nothing. Eventually, Davenport will talk to someone who knows someone who might know something and then finally, a finale. As far as actual stories go, there's just not much here. Sandford always had great skills when writing thrillers and even in dud after dud, these books are very easy reads and I keep up with them not only out of pure nostalgia and the hopes that the author may some day get his groove back and start caring again but also because even when nothing's happening, they're still so damn easy to read. Like that imaginary sitcom I can picture Lucas starring in as a befuddled, clueless dad, a Sandford Prey novel is still somehow comforting to me. Unfortunately, I couldn't recommend this one less. Gosh, do I miss Kidd and Luanne.

This is my first Patterson and definitely my last, and I was hoping for some authenticity in a novel about a former president co-written by an actual former president but there was nothing there that couldn’t be gleaned from any awful TV show or action movie.

1.5 stars. Unlike most of the reviewers, I found this book wanting on so many ways. The beginning was extremely confusing in that the opening bounced between three different periods. The Inspector Gamache series started having major stumbles with All the Devil's are Here, when Gamache, a policeman for a Canadian province, goes to Paris. When local police go international, it's usually a sign the author is running out of ideas (the weapon of mass destruction and the plot to blow the dam were also far fetched, but charmingly so). The Madness of Crowds showed further signs of decline when the main thrust centered around a "statistical analysis" that garners the attention of the public at large. As a statistician, I can tell you that that is one of the most far-fetched fictional plot devices I've come across among any genre of fiction (more believable would be dragons flying spaceships). Additionally, the disagreement among experts seems to not be on the analysis, which is never described, but only on the implications. What was in this "statistical analysis" that is never actually described? An argument for letting the old, sick, and feeble die. That's right... a large portion of the Canadian public as been whipped into a supporting frenzy by a statistical analysis purporting to *show* it would be best to withhold care from those who need it. And now we come to A World of Curiosities, with pages that preach and scold, poor dialogue, dumb characters, recycled plot devices, and one of the most ridiculous villain reveal. I won't comment much on the social themes the book addresses (Penny did much better in the early books), but suffice it to say that most of it is tangential (or completely unrelated) to the narrative, adds nothing to the characters or plot, knowingly distorts history, and tries to have you believe that we have made no progress in social morality since at least the days of the witch hunts in the 1600s, but somehow, at the same time, the book's characters are able to look back on history and pronounce "those people" evil with the confidence of someone who *knows* they would act differently if placed in similar circumstances. I am surprised the editors let so much through and that other readers haven't noticed. ***The rest of the rant will include spoilers... you've been warned*** Inspector Gamache is just plain stupid in this book. Upon discovering that John Flemming (the serial killer from a previous book) has escaped from the Special Handling Unit, what does he do? Any normal human being and especially any wise sage of a policeman as Gamache has been portrayed up to this point would inform the head of the police and there would ensue one of the biggest manhunts in Canadian history. Instead, he decides to keep this fact to himself and only share it with a few close confidants!? (keyboards really should have interrobang keys) There is only one moment when there is any hint of a wider response when Jean Guy is coordinating with other law enforcement, but Gamache seems distinctly uninterested. Instead, when someone receives a minor head wound, for some reason Gamache drives him to the hospital rather than pawning that duty off on someone and coordinating the response himself. When Myrna is worried about Harriet, Gamache tells Myrna that they can track Harriet's phone. Are you kidding me?! She is reportedly in the B&B a stone's throw away. Go knock on the damn door! Circling back to Flemming's escape: he bribes a corrupt prison official to let him go and to put a look-a-like in his cell. This was used as a plot device in a previous book. Finding something hidden in the wall: this was a plot device in a previous book. An entire precinct of corrupt cops (this one centered around half of them being pedos): been done many times in this series. Clues found in a painting: that's from book one. When Gamache decides to send his family away for safety, it is to a lake house that is just a short drive from Three Pines. Come on now. I would've told Reine Marie to just stay at the airport, and I certainly wouldn't have gathered my entire family in one spot that was close to where the killer would be. And Gamache had seen Flemming just a few years prior and knew what he looked like. Why was there confusion over who was and was not Flemming? Flemming ends up being the village minister, though two other old men are thrown in the mix to keep you guessing. Gamache couldn't recognize that the minister was Flemming because he had worn contacts and grew a beard?! Flemming was in Three Pines for for a while serving as the minister without being recognized by anyone! The deeper explanation given for Gamache's inability to recognize Flemming: Flemming had shown love and care to his wife while she slowly died of cancer... that love covered all the evil inside Flemming... that's why Gamache didn't recognize him... [insert puking emoji]. Ruth was right, Gamache really is Clouseau. And if there were a drinking game that said take a shot when "ça va bien aller" or the nodding of Ruth's duck was mentioned, that game would send participants to the hospital with alcohol poisoning. The series had a good run, but this is last book for me.

Yeah, all that creepy stuff was on video, too. Then we move along to the memory of a mass shooting of female engineering students in Montreal. (which did actually occur in 1989) On to an evil serial killer caught by Gamache who has been locked up in a maximum security prison for violent criminals but connives his way out with the help of a corrupt warden and guard who replace him with a lookalike?!?!?! Turns out he is the father of the prostitute’s son (also a psycho) and he has been hiding in plain sight in Three Pines disguised as the pastor of the church. So, he was playing the long game, you see, and went to divinity school after escaping prison so he could infiltrate Three Rivers to get to Gamache. Several murders later he has a knife to Reine-Marie’s throat in front of Gamache in their house and blah, blah blah. Bad guy gets killed and Gamache and Reine-Marie go to their lake house for a month where they are magically healed by the presence of their children and grandchildren. They burn some sage in their house and sing a round of Kumbaya with the locals and all is good again. Ugh! There’s more that bothered me but those are the low points. Time to find a new mystery writer.

** spoiler alert ** I used to be a fan of this author but felt her books were getting darker. Then this book came out. I am I’m so disappointed in how disturbing this book was. There should have been a warning about the nature of the story. As a therapist, I worry about people reading this, and retrigger in their own traumas, and as a former fan, I am so disenchanted with this author and her books.

** spoiler alert ** First, I want to start with a list of WTF‘s from this book. Bear in mind, I’m a person who has read Penny’s books and given them all five star ratings until “A Better Man”. Generally, I feel the books have gotten significantly worse since ABM, but with this book, Penny and Gamache finally “jumped the shark” (or Steak Frites with seasoned fries, if you don’t care for fish). We are asked to believe that Gamache and his wife have had a convicted murderer living with them for 10 years. Ten years that span throughout the previous books. This person, Fiona, was arrested by Gamache then paroled; now she’s living with them and being raised like family. And this is the first we’ve heard of it? And Myrna…Penny had managed to make her a legitimate character, but here Myrna is stripped of everything from personality to physical description. We’ve always known Myrna as being a big woman. In fact, she’s very big. The first 12 books, Myrna’s physical make up was just as comforting as her intellect and ability to love and care for others. But after ABM, Myrna loses all of that depth because Penny can’t stop mentioning Myrna’s “round, ample bottom”, and manages to turn Myrna into a caricature. For the first time in the series, Penny decides to not mention anything about Myrna’s physical presence, but instead blesses her with a niece named Harriet, with whom she’s supposedly close, Penny tries her hardest to make Harriet mysterious and suspicious. But It’s so poorly executed that readers are left feeling betrayed. How could we not know this about Gamache and Myrna? What in the world was Penny thinking? The next mind blowing antic from Penny is the character of Myrna’s boyfriend, Billy Williams. Billy has gone from some type of town caretaker whose one (very funny, in prior books) quality is that no one can understand what he saying. In fact, his general incoherency was used as a plot device in an earlier novel! Here, he speaks in full sentences and he even touches on philosophy. No explanation for this. It just happened. Next , how many times can you use a plot device that involves a look-alike who has (voluntarily! ) been incarcerated in place of the perpetrator? She used it once, many books ago, and then we’re asked to believe it happens again, this time, with a serial killer. I have no affiliation with, nor any particular affection for Canada, but if I were Canadian, I’d be a little peeved and a bit offended. Are Canadians so dense that a corrupt cop, and then a serial killer, could pull the same switcheroo? Penny seems to think (then tries to convince the reader) if you pay enough money, a maximum security prison housing the worst and most dangerous offenders IN THE ENTIRE COUNTRY, will turn a blind eye to the understudies of a corrupt cop and the nation’s worst serial killer. Not that I can totally blame Penny for thinking that enough money and connections could purchase someone to serve a life sentence for another’s crimes… Hillary Clinton is her ‘bestie’. Lastly, Penny and her preaching. Preacher Penny brought up a mass shooting that happened at an engineering college in Canada. This was an actual event in ‘89 described by wiki as an “antifeminist mass shooting”. Since this book is set over 25 years after the tragedy, and none of the characters have any connection to the incident, I couldn’t figure out why Penny devoted so much time to it at the beginning, before it disappeared. Then, I got about a quarter of the way through, and realized it was only to serve as Penny’s gun control platform. From gun control, Canada’s mistreatment of indigenous peoples, racism, etc., PP makes sure she ticks all of the “politically correct and woke” boxes, ad nauseam. The worst is Penny’s regurgitation of every feminist manifesto with lines like “They looked at each other in horror at the crimes committed against women for merely exhibiting intelligence and having a uterus”. Gak. At the end (which couldn’t come quickly enough) the plot holes became more significant than the actual book. The grammar is atrocious. I believe Penny has gone from writing her books to dictating them, verbally, for someone else to type. I began reading passages containing the worst fractures out loud, and it sounded different-much better! I now understand why the fragments are so terrible. She’s dictating the book and they are transcribing it almost verbatim. The rush to get the book out was more important, obviously, and letting a really good editor loose on it. I’ve spent a great deal of time on this review; more time than would be justified for ANY book, good or bad. It’s just rare that I’ve been so enamored, charmed and wholly consumed by a series, only to have it go so badly, so quickly. But to this day, I would encourage the reading of her first 12 books…Just stop at 12. Because I can assure you that there’s nothing worse than traveling with these characters for so many years, then having it plunge into some farcical, insulting slap in the face. It’s in this that the true crime takes place Or, to use Penny’s words, it’s… “A crime. One of betrayal. Absolute. And wholly. To be considered personal. And finding that truth. Here. And now.” Sigh.

I am so disappointed in this book. Rather than a murder mystery involving interesting characters, this is all about politics - and I don't want politics invading my pleasure reading. Because I follow Ms. Penny, I am aware of her political leanings, but the fact that she had to bring them into her books is so sad to me. It made the story much less interesting, as it was trying to get its message across (orange man bad) rather than just trying to tell a good story. I found it convoluted and often difficult to follow. I really hope, if she continues this series, that she returns to her original style and leaves the politics out of it.

I’m sorry Louise Penny. I’ve been a huge fan of yours since I first read your books in 2016. I’ve read them all, in order. I’ve met you three times, first at Manior Hovey in Quebec, soon after your husband’s death, and (i later found out) were planning a party for Hilary Clinton at that beautiful hotel. You were absolutely delightful. But I don’t like your stories anymore. I’m not interested in elaborate conspiracies, where everyone seems to change from being a bad guy to a good guy and back again. Where the story line is so ridiculously complex, and involves whole continents, and all of mankind and yet, only Armand can figure it out. Nope, I didn’t like The Grey Wolf, and I did not like this book. I won’t read your books again. I miss the early ones, with poetry, and food, and a warm fire, and a simple murder in the woods.

This is my first DNF on Goodreads. This in spite of glowing reviews from regulars comments that I respect. The only reason I started this book is that I’ve enjoyed her earlier ones. I liked CHIEF INSPECTOR ARMAND GAMACHE and the characters in village of Three Pines. They were charming and humorous with clever puzzles to solve. And the Author’s Note intrigued me. It did get me to look up some facts for 2025... they don't reflect her POV for content of her story. Since I was so very disappointed, I don't have any praise for this and would not recommend reading. I think I'll stop reading Penny as long as her novels have turned to dark political views. The world is forever changing and people are stressed with trying to keep up. Some novelists are venting through their storytelling and not helping anything. Not even giving us respite with a good story.

An utterly disappointing end to what was a wonderful series. Somewhere in the last several books she changed gears from cozy, wry and intellectual mysteries to over-the-top political thrillers with a heavy dose of patronizing lectures on climate change and societal behavior. She also took so many liberties with police procedure it made much of the book a joke, and don't even get me started on the multiple red herrings and twists near the end that, rather than heighten the intrigue, made the entire book confusing and plain ridiculous. I should have listened to other reviews and stopped after The Grey Wolf (or possibly before) but I've learned my lesson, if there are even more installments I will not subject myself to this punishment again.

Terrible! Not funny. Not original. Waste of money. Waste of time. The Stephanie Plum series is grasping, and Evanovich keeps hacking her way through. Subpar on every level.

Game On, book #28 in the Stephanie Plum series is a huge disappointment. As a dedicated fan, I have read and listened to every book in this series and there is no way this was written by Evanovich. The lack of cursing from anyone, but especially from Lula, means the author does not in any way understand Lula’s (previously) hilarious character. There’s very little joking around and I don’t think I laughed once the entire book. Stephanie, Lula, and Connie’s conversations and ridiculously funny scenarios are what drive the books, and this was completely missing from #28. The whole thing has been “PG-ed” and in doing so, all the laughable, lively interplay between characters is gone. Stephanie’s mother stops drinking to knit and philosophize. Grandma seems normal. Joe becomes a sweet doormat. The tension between Ranger, Joe, and Stephanie is gone, replaced with lackluster Diesel (he’s really the worst). We don’t need Diesel in any of the books and I was disappointed to find he was the main man character in Game On. It would be more appropriately titled Game Off. There are no gut busting lines from Lula, no hot-headed Morelli scenes, no Grandma hijinks, and the side characters are equally boring. In the audiobook, Grandma Mazur’s name is even mispronounced and I am sure Lorelei King remembers how to pronounce it (King is awesome!). She must have been directed to say MAY-zor rather than Maah-zur. This may seem trivial but it is a whole book worth of these contradictions and departures from the series. None of the characters act like themselves in the book. Even though I suspect some of the previous 27 books have been ghost written, I have loved every book until now. I hope the series continues written by Evanovich or a ghost writer who actually understands the characters and their quirks and can showcase them. It is one of the joys in my life to read a new Plum book every year or so, but if this same person writes the next book I will stop reading/listening to this series.
